00

How to use this · 白話版

學習地圖與術語

What this is

A self-directed curriculum that takes you from the income/spread basics you already know up a MECE ladder to the professional layer — trading volatility itself and reading dealer flows. Work it one concept at a time: read the teardown, study the visual, read the real case, then run the fit-target screen to pick a stock and place a small, logged trade. The Verify-your-edge capstone is the loop that turns each concept into a measured experiment.

How to read a module

Every strategy is torn down in 8 facets: ① 機制 how it is built · ② 損益 the payoff (live, Black-Scholes rendered: solid = at expiry, dashed teal = at ~half the life left) · ③ 希臘字母 its Greek signature · ④ 適用情境 the one regime it is built for · ⑤ 真實案例 a verified case · ⑥ 進場/管理/出場 the parameter grid + defend/roll table + a worked ticket · ⑦ 選標的 a runnable fit screen · ⑧ 風險與卡點 what blows it up + an honest-edge verdict.

Make the charts yours: every payoff and Greek diagram is live — drag the spot / IV / DTE sliders beneath it to scrub the position and watch the curves and Greeks respond. Solid line = P/L at expiry; dashed teal = P/L marked to the current IV & days-remaining — drag DTE down to watch theta bleed and IV to watch vega, while the entry ticket and frame stay fixed. Chart axes are per share; multiply by 100 for one contract. Fastest way to build feel.

Chart limits (read once): the engine is a single-expiry Black-Scholes approximation — flat IV, no dividends, no borrow cost, European exercise. Perfect for intuition, but for multi-expiry trades (calendars, PMCC, diagonals) and for assignment / ex-dividend / pin questions, treat the curves as illustrative, not exact; the prose in those modules is the authority.

The honest-edge tags

Genuine a real, repeatable retail edge · Skill-dependent positive only with discipline/execution · Crowded low edge after costs/competition · Pro-only needs infrastructure most retail lacks. The point is to find where your remaining edge is, not to admire theory.

Before you place ANY trade — verify on a live chain

Every ticket, screen, IVR figure, and candidate ticker in this dashboard is an illustrative TEMPLATE (reconstructed / est., as-of 2026-06-17) — not a live quote. Options reprice constantly; skew, IVR, and earnings dates move. This is a learning & structuring tool, not an execution terminal: re-derive every number from your own broker's live chain before risking a cent. Run this refresh first:

Pre-trade refresh: date/time · spot · IVR & IV-vs-HV · skew / term structure · bid/ask, mid & spread % · OI / volume · earnings or ex-div inside the window? · chosen strikes / DTE / delta · net credit or debit · max loss & BPR · POP · profit target · stop / roll trigger · time-stop (e.g. 21 DTE) · position size (% of account). If you cannot fill every field from live data — or only have delayed/free GEX — stand down.

Trade journal schema (copy these columns — this is the Verify-your-edge loop)

date · ticker · strategy · thesis · entry_IV · IVR · HV · expected_move · strikes/DTE · credit_or_debit · max_loss · BPR · POP · size_% · exit_date · exit_PL · realized_move · IV_crush · slippage_vs_mid · P50_vs_outcome · tag/lesson. Review a strategy after ~30 logged trades (50+ for short-premium); only trust an edge estimate near ~200 (negative skew makes small samples lie).

Glossary — decode the jargon

IV / IVR — implied volatility; IV Rank = where today's IV sits in its 1-yr range (0–100). High IVR favors selling premium.
The Greeks — Δ delta (direction), Γ gamma (how delta changes), Θ theta (time decay), V vega (vol sensitivity), ρ rho (rates).
DTE — days to expiration. POP — probability of profit. EV — expected value (POP × win − loss-prob × loss).
Assignment / pin — being forced to buy/sell shares; pin = price sitting at a short strike into expiry, leaving assignment uncertain.
BPR — buying-power reduction: capital your broker holds against an undefined-risk position.
VRP — variance risk premium: implied vol tends to exceed realized vol, the structural pay for selling vol.
Gamma scalp — being long gamma and re-hedging delta to harvest realized movement against the theta you pay.
GEX / DEX — estimated dealer gamma / delta exposure by strike; an inference used as a regime filter, not a signal.
Vanna / charm — second-order Greeks: how delta moves with vol (vanna) and with time (charm); drive dealer re-hedging.
Term structure — vol across maturities. Note: the VIX-futures curve ≠ the SPX-options IV curve.
Skew / smile — IV differing by strike; equity puts trade richer (crash-o-phobia).
0DTE / OPEX / max pain — same-day options; monthly expiration; the (dubious) strike that maximizes option-holder loss.
Dispersion — trading index vol vs the vol of its members; fundamentally a correlation trade.
PMCC — poor-man's covered call: a deep-ITM LEAPS call standing in for 100 shares under a short call.

The mastery map — where the remaining edge hides

精通地圖

Most retail attention clusters in the income tier (covered calls, the wheel). The less-crowded skill — trading volatility itself, and reading dealer flows as a regime filter (context, not a standalone edge) — is where the remaining asymmetry and most of this dashboard's depth sit. Climb the tree; each node lists its prerequisites. Honest caveat: the L4 flow concepts are a lens on the market's mechanics, not a retail money-printer.

Options mastery map: a nine-node Foundations spine (F0–F8) feeds four prerequisite tiers — L1 Core (long-premium vs short-premium income, where most retail crowds), L2 Spreads, L3 Vol, and L4 Flows (pro-only/crowded regime context, not a retail edge). Only strategies with an actual module are shown.
Options awareness curve — attention piles into the CROWD zone (income/wheel, covered calls and cash-secured puts) where edge is already consensus-priced; thins through the SLOPE (spreads, IV/calendar trades at recognition stage) and trails into the thin EDGE tail (vol dispersion, gamma/structure, order flow) where skill-based asymmetry still remains. The play is to move down the tail before a strategy crowds.
02

Foundations — the operating layer (read first)

基礎·操作層

These are the prerequisites the whole curriculum leans on — the Greeks, implied vol & the surface, put-call parity, assignment/settlement mechanics, the bid-ask edge, sizing & margin, where to get data free, and tax. Skipping these is what turns "a good idea" into a blown-up account.

Foundation

Greeks Primitives 希臘字母入門

The minimum vocabulary before L1: what you actually pay for, and the single axis — long-vol vs short-vol — that splits every strategy in this dashboard.

What it is

An option price has exactly two parts. Intrinsic value is what it's worth if expiry were right now: for a call, max(S − K, 0); for a put, max(K − S, 0). Extrinsic value (a.k.a. time value) is everything on top of that — the price you pay for the chance the option finishes further in the money before it dies. Extrinsic value is the entire battleground of options trading: it is what decays, what inflates before earnings, and what you are either renting (as a buyer) or collecting rent on (as a seller). At expiry, extrinsic value is always exactly 0 — every option converges to pure intrinsic.

Key math / intuition

Delta (Δ) = how much the option price moves per $1 move in the underlying. A 0.50-delta call gains ~$0.50 if the stock rises $1; it is also a rough (and overstated) gut-check on probability of finishing ITM. Long calls have Δ between 0 and +1; long puts between −1 and 0. Theta (Θ) = how much extrinsic value bleeds away per day, all else equal. If you are long an option, Θ is your enemy (you paid for time that is draining); if you are short, Θ is your paycheck. Crucially, decay is not linear: for an ATM option, extrinsic value scales roughly with the square root of time remaining (√T), so the per-day decay rate (∝ 1/√T) accelerates into expiry — which is why the last few weeks of an ATM option's life are where most extrinsic value evaporates.

ATM extrinsic (time) value follows a √T shape: it bleeds slowly and near-linearly when expiry is far away, then the per-day decay rate steepens sharply in the final ~30 days because theta is proportional to 1/√T, reaching zero at expiration (DTE counts down left-to-right).

The one axis that organizes everything — vega sign

Every strategy in this dashboard sits on one side of a single line: are you long volatility or short volatility? Vega is the Greek that measures sensitivity to a 1-point change in implied vol (IV). The sign of your net vega is your whole identity.

Long-vol / long-premium (Θ-negative, debit): you BUY options. You own gamma and vega, you pay theta every day, and you need a move (in price, or in IV itself) bigger than what the market priced in. Long calls, long puts, long straddles. Time is against you; a big surprise rescues you.

Short-vol / short-premium (Θ-positive, credit): you SELL options. You collect theta daily and you're betting the realized move stays smaller than the implied move that was priced in. Covered calls, cash-secured puts, the Wheel, credit spreads, condors. Time is your ally; a violent move is the thing that hurts you. This is exactly why L1 is split by vega sign and not lumped under "premium & direction" — a long call and a covered call are mirror images on the one axis that matters most. (Caveat carried forward to F1: a covered call's dominant exposure is its long-stock delta, not its small short-vol overlay — the vega-sign axis classifies it, but does not describe what most of its P&L is.)

How to read it / where to get it free

Every retail broker chain shows per-contract Δ and Θ (Robinhood, Fidelity, Schwab/thinkorswim, IBKR, Tastytrade — all free). Read Δ as "shares-equivalent per contract × 100" (a +0.40 call ≈ being long 40 shares). Read Θ as dollars-per-day-per-contract once you multiply by 100. For a clean visual of decay and the long/short-vol split, the option-payoff and Greek plots on this dashboard are rendered live — nudge the IV and DTE inputs and watch the curves move.

The gotcha

Delta is not a true probability — it is a risk-neutral, model-derived sensitivity (a call's Δ ≈ N(d1) sits above N(d2), the model's own P(finish ITM)) that systematically overstates the real-world chance of finishing ITM, especially for OTM options where skew inflates put deltas. And the most expensive beginner mistake hides in the vega axis: traders buy long-dated calls "to give the thesis time," not realizing they've taken a Θ-negative, long-vol position that loses money every single quiet day even when they're right on direction but the move is too slow. Knowing which side of the vol axis you're on — before you click buy — is the entire point of F0.

Foundation

The Greeks 希臘字母（全）

The full sensitivity hinge — Δ Γ Θ V ρ, position Greeks, and a P&L attribution that makes "why long options lose" and "a covered call is a long-delta bet" quantitative rather than asserted.

What it is

The Greeks are partial derivatives of an option's price with respect to each thing that can move it. Five matter. Delta (Δ) — price sensitivity to spot. Gamma (Γ) — how fast Δ itself changes as spot moves; it is the curvature, the second derivative, and it is what makes long options convex (your Δ grows in your favor and shrinks against you). Theta (Θ) — decay of extrinsic value per day. Vega (V) — sensitivity to a 1-point change in implied vol. Rho (ρ) — sensitivity to interest rates. Position Greeks are simply per-contract Greeks × contracts × 100, summed across legs and (for delta) including any stock — that aggregate is what your account actually feels. Sign convention to memorize: a long call is +Δ +Γ −Θ +V; a long put is −Δ +Γ −Θ +V; a short put is +Δ −Γ +Θ −V; a short call is −Δ −Γ +Θ −V. Every option you buy is long gamma and pays theta; every option you sell is short gamma and collects theta.

Key math / intuition — how each shifts with spot, time, IV

Δ drifts toward ±1 as an option goes deep ITM and toward 0 deep OTM; an ATM option sits near 0.50. Γ is largest for ATM, short-dated options and collapses for far ITM/OTM — meaning your delta is most unstable exactly at-the-money near expiry (the source of pin risk and gamma scalping). Θ and Γ are joined at the hip with opposite signs: you cannot own gamma without paying theta, and you cannot collect theta without being short gamma. That single trade-off is the engine of the entire dashboard. V is highest for ATM, longer-dated options — LEAPS are vega monsters; 0DTE options barely have vega. As expiry approaches, Γ and Θ blow up while V shrinks.

A long call's delta rises from near 0 to near +1 as spot climbs through the strike — that S-curve is gamma in picture form:

Rho, rates, dividends & carry — why this matters again post-2022

For a decade of zero rates, rho was a rounding error and everyone ignored it. With short rates having sat well above 4% in 2023–2025, carry is back and it quietly reshapes the chain. Higher rates lift call prices and depress put prices (you can defer paying for stock via a call, and the forward sits above spot). This is the mechanism behind three real-world effects: (1) put-call parity — C − P = S − PV(K) − PV(div) — only balances once you discount the strike and subtract dividends; (2) early exercise of American calls becomes economically rational only just before an ex-dividend date when the dividend captured exceeds the remaining time value, which is the classic surprise-assignment trap for covered-call and PMCC sellers; and (3) LEAPS pricing bakes in years of carry, so a deep-ITM LEAPS call is partly a financed stock position — you're effectively borrowing at the implied rate embedded in the option. Dividends pull the whole call surface down and the put surface up by roughly the PV of expected dividends to expiry.

P&L attribution / Greeks over time — the quantitative payoff

Your daily P&L decomposes, to first order, as: ΔP ≈ Δ·dS + ½Γ·dS² + Θ·dt + V·dIV + ρ·dr. This identity settles two arguments cleanly. Why long options usually lose: the buyer's Θ·dt term bleeds every day and is certain, while the ½Γ·dS² rescue term only pays when the realized move squared exceeds what the priced IV was charging — and since IV runs a few points rich on average (the variance risk premium; see F2), the gamma rarely earns back the theta. Why a covered call is a long-delta bet, not a "short-vol bet": run the attribution on long 100 shares + short one 30-delta call. Net position delta ≈ +100 − 30 = +70; the short call contributes maybe −0.05 position vega and a few dollars of positive theta. The dominant term by an order of magnitude is Δ·dS — you are ~70% long the stock, with the upside capped at the short strike. The short-vol overlay is real but small. That is why a covered call hurts in both tails: in a big rally your gains are capped at the strike (you forfeit the run above it), and in a big selloff the premium cushions only a sliver while the long-stock delta takes the full hit — if the name drops 20%, the credit offsets a fraction and the delta does the rest of the damage.

How to read it / where to get it free

thinkorswim's "Analyze" tab, Tastytrade, and IBKR all show net position Greeks for a whole spread or portfolio — read those, not single-contract Greeks, because the book-level net delta and net vega are what get you margin-called. For rho/carry intuition, compare a deep-ITM call's price to (stock − strike): the gap is mostly financing. OptionStrat and the live Greek plots on this dashboard let you scrub spot/time/IV and watch each Greek morph.

The gotcha

Greeks are local and instantaneous — they're accurate for a small move right now and lie for big, fast, or vol-shifting moves, because gamma changes the delta and vanna/charm (F-series second-order Greeks) change everything during stress. The classic blow-up: a seller looks at a comfortable −0.10 position delta, the underlying gaps 8% overnight, gamma flips the delta to −0.60, vega losses stack on top, and the "neutral" book is suddenly a runaway directional loss. Treat Greeks as a snapshot to re-take constantly, never a static promise.

Foundation

Implied Vol & the Surface 隱含波動率與曲面

IV is the price of an option restated as a number — and the single most misused metric in retail options: high IV Rank alone is not edge, and the negatively-skewed P&L is the tail that quietly eats the year.

What it is

Implied volatility (IV) is the volatility input that, plugged into a pricing model (Black-Scholes), reproduces the option's current market price. It is not a forecast you can trust — it is the market's quoted price for vol, an annualized one-standard-deviation move the option is charging for. Realized (historical) volatility is what the stock actually did, measured ex-post — typically the annualized standard deviation of daily log returns (close-to-close) over a window, or a higher-fidelity estimator (Parkinson, Garman-Klass) that uses the high/low range. The gap between the two — implied minus subsequent realized — is the variance risk premium, and it is the deepest reason short-vol strategies have a structural tailwind. Crucially, that premium is compensation for being short gamma — i.e., short realized-vol/jump risk — not a payment for free; vega risk is a separate axis you can often hedge or neutralize.

Key math / intuition — IVR, IV vs forward realized, and why IVR alone is not edge

IV Rank = where today's IV sits between its 52-week low and high, as a percent: (IV − IV_low) / (IV_high − IV_low). IV Percentile = the fraction of trading days over the past year that IV closed below today's level — usually the more honest of the two because one freak spike doesn't anchor the whole scale. High IVR tells you options are expensive relative to this name's own recent past. That is necessary but nowhere near sufficient. The trap: IV is high precisely because the market expects a bigger forward move — earnings, an FDA decision, a macro print. Selling that rich IV is only edge if the IV is rich relative to what the stock will actually realize, not relative to its own history. A 90 IVR into a binary event can still be a terrible sale if the realized move blows through it. Edge lives in the IV-vs-forward-realized spread, which you can only estimate — IVR is a screening filter, not the signal itself.

The variance risk premium is not free money — it is compensation for a fat left tail (the price of being short gamma when a jump comes). Historically, broad-index implied volatility has tended to exceed subsequently realized volatility a clear majority of the time — commonly cited at roughly ~80–85% of rolling windows, by an average on the order of ~3–4 volatility points (est.; magnitude varies by index, window, and period — e.g. VIX-vs-S&P-realized studies, CFA Institute / OptionStrat; as-of mid-2026, treat as directional not precise). That asymmetric split is the entire short-vol bargain: you win small and often, and lose big and rarely.

Smile, skew & the surface (awareness only)

Black-Scholes assumes one flat volatility for all strikes; the market doesn't. Plot IV against strike and you get a smile (both wings richer than ATM) or, for equity indices, a skew/smirk — downside puts carry materially higher IV than upside calls, because crashes are faster and more correlated than rallies and everyone is bidding for crash insurance. Extend it across expiries and you get the volatility surface. You don't trade the surface yet (that's L3 skew/term-structure work) — but you must recognize it, because it means "the IV" of a name is really a different number at every strike and date. Note: this is the option term structure (SPX implied vol by expiry), which is what a calendar trades — not the VIX-futures term structure (the VX roll / VIX-ETP path), which is a different curve and is covered separately at L3.

Equity options carry a downward-sloping implied-vol skew: out-of-the-money puts trade at higher IV than calls because investors persistently bid downside protection (crash-o-phobia). The two overlays show what happens when spot moves: sticky-strike (blue) keeps IV pinned to each strike so the new ATM slides down the existing curve, while sticky-delta / sticky-moneyness (purple) drags the whole curve along with spot, holding ATM IV constant. Which regime dominates drives realized hedge P&L and the sign of vanna/volga exposure.

POP vs EV, P50, touch-vs-expire — and the tail that eats the year

A high probability of profit (POP) is seductive and nearly meaningless on its own. Selling a 5-delta strangle can show ~90% POP, but the 10% losing tail is so large it can erase a year of the 90% winners — that is negative skew, the structural shape of every short-premium book, and it is why EV (expected value) and tail size matter far more than win-rate. P50 (the probability of ever hitting a 50%-of-max-profit target before expiry) is usually higher than POP and is the metric Tastytrade-style management leans on. Probability of touch ≈ roughly 2× the probability of expiring ITM — a strike gets tested about twice as often as it gets breached at expiry, which is exactly why short-option sellers feel constant pressure and why a static "it'll be fine by expiry" mindset gets people assigned or stopped at the worst moment.

How to read it / where to get it free

IVR / IV Percentile: Tastytrade and Market Chameleon show both free; thinkorswim has IV Percentile built in. Realized vol: compute it yourself from free daily closes (annualized stdev of log returns × √252) or read it off Market Chameleon / a portfolio-margin platform. IV vs realized overlay & term structure: Market Chameleon and barchart.com (free tiers) plot IV30 against HV30 — the single most useful free chart for deciding if vol is genuinely rich. Skew & surface: the broker chain itself (read IV column down the strikes); OptionStrat for a cleaner picture. Always check whether the number is live or end-of-day, and remember IVR is a same-name relative metric — never compare IVR across different tickers as if it were absolute cheapness.

The gotcha

Two model-risk landmines. First, Black-Scholes is wrong on purpose — it assumes lognormal returns, constant vol, no jumps, and continuous hedging; real markets gap, cluster, and fat-tail, so every Greek and every probability you read is conditioned on a model that systematically under-weights disaster. Second, and most important for this whole dashboard: the probabilities on your screen are risk-neutral, not real-world. A "70% POP" is a pricing artifact, not a forecast of reality — it embeds the variance risk premium and the crash skew, so it overstates how often the safe outcome truly happens and understates the cost when it doesn't. The tail that eats the year is the literal mechanism by which a trader with a glowing 85% win-rate ends December down money: a few negatively-skewed losers, each many times the size of a winner, sized as if the screen probabilities were the real ones. Respect the left tail, size for it, and treat IVR as a filter — never as edge.

Foundation

Put-Call Parity & Synthetics 賣買權平價與合成部位

One equation, C − P = S − PV(K) − PV(div), glues calls, puts and stock into the same object — it is the engine behind every "synthetic," every skew reconciliation, and every box/conversion arbitrage.

What it is

Parity says a long call and a short put at the same strike K and same European expiry are a forward on the stock. Rearranged for a dividend-paying name held over the option's life: C − P = S − PV(K) − PV(div), where PV(K) is the strike discounted at the risk-free rate and PV(div) is the present value of dividends paid before expiry. Read it plainly: owning the call and selling the put gives you the stock's upside and downside, minus the cost of carrying it (you don't pay for the stock until expiry, so you keep interest on K, but you forgo the dividends the stockholder collects). Everything else in this dashboard — why a CSP looks like a covered call (L1-3), why a calendar trades term structure not direction (L2-6), why a box is a fixed-rate loan (L4-6) — falls out of this one identity.

Key math / intuition — the 6 synthetics

Move any one term across the equals sign and you get a synthetic version of the position on the other side. Memorize the wheel of six: synthetic long stock = long call + short put (same K); synthetic short stock = short call + long put; synthetic long call = long stock + long put (a married put / protective put); synthetic short call = short stock + short put; synthetic long put = short stock + long call; synthetic short put = long stock + short call (that is a covered call — which is why L1-2 covered call and L1-3 CSP share a payoff). Two riskless cousins exploit when the equation breaks: a conversion = long stock + synthetic short stock (long put, short call) locks in a tiny financing spread when calls are rich; a reversal is the mirror (short stock + long call + short put) when puts are rich. Four legs at two strikes give a box spread = a synthetic zero-coupon loan whose payoff is just the strike width (K₂ − K₁) at expiry — pros trade it to borrow/lend at near-risk-free rates.

Put-call parity synthetics wheel. From the no-arbitrage identity C - P = S - PV(K), the six synthetic equivalences fall out by rearrangement: synthetic long stock (+C -P), synthetic short stock (+P -C), synthetic long/short call (+S +P / -S -P), and synthetic long/short put (+C -S / +S -C). Plus = long, minus = short; carry and dividends shift the PV(K) term.

How to read it / where to get it free

You rarely "compute" parity by hand — you use it as a sanity check and a cheaper-execution router. (1) Pick the liquid leg. If a deep-ITM call has a wide spread but the synthetic (stock + OTM put) is tight, build the synthetic — same exposure, less slippage (ties to F5). (2) Reconcile skew. When an OTM put looks "expensive," parity tells you the same-strike call carries the identical implied vol — skew is a property of the strike, not of calls-vs-puts. (3) Read the dividend/rate fingerprint. If a stock's ATM call is cheaper than its put by more than rates justify, the market is pricing a dividend or hard-to-borrow rate. Free tools: any broker chain (IBKR, Schwab/thinkorswim, Tastytrade) shows call and put mids side by side; OIC (optionseducation.org) has free synthetic-position explainers; the carry inputs (T-bill rate, ex-div dates) come free from CME FedWatch / the company IR calendar. As-of 2026-06-17, with short rates still well off zero, the PV(K) term is large enough that "synthetic stock vs real stock" and "box financing" actually move money — parity is not a textbook curiosity in this rate regime.

The gotcha

Cash settlement is not "no pin." Cash-settled, European index options (SPX/XSP) remove physical share-assignment and early exercise, but they do NOT remove strike-pinning behavior, last-hour gamma whip, or AM vs PM (SOQ) settlement risk — your P/L can still hinge on exactly where the index fixes. Parity is an equality of European payoffs, not of risk. The strict identity holds only for European, dividend-and-rate-adjusted options; on American options early-exercise optionality (F4) and the PV(div) term make "equivalent" structures behave differently in your account. The covered call and the CSP have the same payoff diagram but not the same buying-power reduction, the same assignment object (shares vs cash), or the same dividend/early-exercise exposure — so "CSP = synthetic covered call" is true only under parity and must always carry that qualifier. And the most dangerous trap: a box looks "riskless" on paper but is only riskless with European, cash-settled legs (SPX). Put it on American-style options and an early assignment on one short leg collapses the symmetry — that is exactly what blew up the famous 1R0NYMAN Robinhood trade (see F4 / L4-6). Synthetic ≠ identical; carry, exercise style, and settlement are where the equivalence leaks.

Foundation

Mechanics, Assignment & Pin 機制·指派·釘住

The plumbing that turns a clean payoff diagram into a real position — exercise style, settlement, ex-dividend call assignment, and weekend pin uncertainty — and the single biggest real-world killer of Wheel and short-option traders.

What it is

Once you sell an option you no longer control your own exit — the long holder does. Four mechanical axes decide what actually lands in your account: (1) Exercise style — American (exercisable any time, all single-name equity and ETF options like SPY/QQQ) vs European (only at expiry, broad-based index options like SPX/NDX). (2) Settlement — physical/share-settled (equities, SPY: you get long or short 100 shares) vs cash-settled (SPX: the dollar difference is wired, no shares, no physical-assignment pin). (3) Settlement time — AM-settled (legacy monthly SPX uses the Friday opening print, SET, which can gap away from Thursday's close) vs PM-settled (SPXW weeklies and all equities use the closing price). (4) Auto-exercise — the OCC automatically exercises anything $0.01 or more in-the-money at expiry unless instructed otherwise, so "it expired basically worthless" can still leave you assigned 100 shares per contract over a weekend.

Key math / intuition — ex-dividend call assignment

The one early-assignment case that bites short-call sellers (covered calls, the Wheel's call leg, PMCC short legs, calendars). A rational holder of an American ITM call exercises early only to capture a dividend, and only when the dividend exceeds the call's remaining time value. Intuition: exercising throws away the call's time value but captures the dividend the stockholder receives — so when Dividend > remaining extrinsic value, exercise the night before the ex-date is the optimal play. Practical screen for a short call: it is exposed to ex-div assignment when it is deep ITM, the ex-date falls before expiry, and its extrinsic value has decayed below the dividend (most acute on the last trading day before ex-div). The cost if you get assigned and the stock then drops by the dividend on the ex-date: you can be left short stock owing the dividend, or pulled out of a covered position right before the payout you were counting on. Deep-ITM puts have the mirror logic driven by interest (exercise to capture interest on the strike proceeds), which matters most when rates are high.

Ex-dividend early-assignment decision for a short ITM call. The holder of a long call exercises early (assigning you) only when it pays to forfeit the call's remaining extrinsic value to capture the dividend — i.e. when extrinsic &lt; dividend. Left: extrinsic ($0.85) still exceeds the dividend ($0.40), so the call is held and you are safe. Right: extrinsic has decayed to $0.05, below the $0.40 dividend, so the rational holder exercises the day before ex-date and you are assigned, ending up short the stock and owing the dividend. Watch ITM short calls whose time value collapses below the payout as ex-date approaches.

How to read it / where to get it free

Run a mechanics checklist before every short-premium trade. (1) Mark the ex-date. Free from the company IR page, Nasdaq.com dividend calendar, or your broker's symbol overview — if an ex-date sits inside your DTE and your short call could go deep ITM, plan to roll or close before it. (2) Watch extrinsic vs dividend. thinkorswim, IBKR and Tastytrade all show extrinsic/time value per contract; when extrinsic on your short ITM call drops under the upcoming dividend, assume assignment. (3) Know your settlement. Confirm AM vs PM (SPX monthly = AM/SET; SPXW = PM) and physical vs cash on the contract spec page — Cboe publishes these free. (4) Manage the weekend. Anything that could finish ITM and that you do not want assigned must be closed before Friday's close, because auto-exercise plus Monday-open gap risk turns a $0.05 option into 100 shares of unhedged delta over two non-trading days. Hard-to-borrow names compound this: if you're assigned short stock you can't borrow, you face buy-in and borrow fees — check borrow rates (IBKR shows them free) before selling puts/calls on small, heavily-shorted names.

The gotcha

Pin risk is the real, unavoidable hazard; max pain is folklore — do not confuse them. Pin risk is genuine assignment uncertainty when the stock closes right at your short strike into expiry: you don't know until after hours whether you'll be assigned, so you wake up either flat or holding ±100 shares of naked weekend delta. That is a true exposure (L4-4). "Max pain" — the strike where the most option value expires worthless — is a crowded, often post-hoc heuristic with weak predictive power; treat it as trivia, not a target. The Wheel-killer lesson: a "safe" short put or covered call can hand you shares (or pull your shares) at the worst moment via ex-div or pin, and the only structural escape is cash-settled European index options (SPX) — no shares, no early exercise, no physical-assignment pin, no ex-div. This is also exactly why the 1R0NYMAN box "could not go tits up" but did: American-style legs got assigned early and unwound the supposedly riskless box (L4-6). Mechanics, not your thesis, is what most often blows up short-premium accounts.

Foundation

Liquidity & Execution — the Market-Maker Seat 流動性與成交

The bid/ask spread is the price you pay for the liquidity you take, and on most retail multi-leg trades it is the single largest and most certain cost you will ever incur — bigger than commissions, more certain than your edge.

What it is

Every quote is set by a market maker (MM) who must be paid to warehouse risk. When you cross the spread to get filled now, you hand the MM the half-spread as compensation — that is a guaranteed loss on entry, before the market moves a tick. The cost stacks: a four-leg iron condor crosses four spreads on the way in and four on the way out. If each leg's spread is $0.10 wide and you pay mid-to-natural on all eight crossings, you can bleed $0.40+ per side — on a trade collecting a $1.50 credit, that is a quarter of your gross edge surrendered to execution before theta does anything. The hierarchy of certainty matters: your directional/vol thesis is a maybe; the bid/ask cost is a certainty. Sizing this correctly is what separates a strategy that backtests well from one that survives a real account.

Key math / intuition — the cost stack

Decompose what a fill actually costs. (1) Spread. Measure it as a fraction of mid: spread ÷ mid. Under 5% is tradable, over 10–15% is a tax you cannot beat. (2) Slippage. The gap between the mid you targeted and the price you got — worst at the open/close, on wide strikes, and in fast tape. (3) Commissions. Typically $0.50–$0.65 per contract; on a 4-leg condor that is ~$5 round-trip — small versus spread, but real. (4) Order type. A market order pays the full spread plus any sweep; a limit at the mid (or mid-minus-a-penny working in) often gets filled by an MM willing to better the NBBO, capturing part of the spread back. Always use limits on options; never market-order a multi-leg. (5) Liquidity gates. Demand open interest > 100–500 per strike and daily volume that supports your size, so you can exit without becoming the only seller. Time-of-day: avoid the first and last ~15 minutes when spreads are widest and the MM is least generous.

All-in cost of a retail multi-leg option trade as a stacked bar: the bid/ask spread crossed across legs (red) is by far the largest and most certain cost, then slippage on size (gold), then commissions/fees (dim). The dashed teal "your expected edge" line sits only slightly above the top of the stack, making the point visceral: the spread is the market-maker's edge and the most certain cost you pay, and your theoretical edge must clear the entire friction stack before a multi-leg options trade is profitable.

How to read it / where to get it free — SPX vs SPY

The clean place to see all of this is the SPX-vs-SPY choice. Both track the S&P 500, but the plumbing differs in ways that compound. SPX: European, cash-settled, broad-based index — so no physical-assignment pin or early assignment (cash-settled) (F4), and it qualifies for Section 1256 60/40 tax treatment (60% long-term / 40% short-term regardless of holding period; see F8). Its multiplier is $100 per index point, so one SPX contract carries roughly ~10× the notional of one SPY contract (≈$600k est. per SPX contract vs ≈$60k est. per SPY contract, since SPX's $100 multiplier sits on an index level ~10× SPY's share price). That means one SPX contract replaces ~10 SPY contracts — one spread crossed instead of ten, slashing execution cost on size. SPY: American, share-settled, an ETF — carries pin/assignment risk, pays dividends (ex-div early-call-assignment risk), is taxed as a normal equity (all short-term if held <1yr), but has penny-wide strikes and tiny per-contract size that suit small accounts. Where to read liquidity free: any broker chain shows bid/ask/mid, OI and volume per strike; Cboe.com and the OCC publish contract specs and settlement type; Market Chameleon and barchart.com show option liquidity scores and average spreads free. Rule of thumb as-of 2026-06-17: once your S&P trade is more than ~5 SPY-equivalent lots, SPX usually wins on execution + tax + no-assignment combined.

The gotcha

You will lose more money to the spread you ignore than to the thesis you got wrong. Retail traders obsess over picking direction and IV regime, then surrender the edge at the fill — paying the natural on illiquid weeklies, market-ordering condors, trading $0.20-wide strikes on a thinly-traded name. The MM is not your enemy; the spread is the rent on immediacy, and you control whether you pay full price (market order, bad time of day, illiquid chain) or claw most of it back (limit at mid, liquid underlying, mid-day, fewer legs). The asymmetry is brutal: a wide spread costs you on entry and exit and on every defensive roll, so an illiquid trade taxes you three-plus times for the same position. Before you ever think about Greeks, gate the underlying on liquidity — OI, volume, spread% of mid — because the most certain number in your whole P&L is the one you give away crossing the bid/ask.

Foundation

Risk, Sizing & Margin 風險·部位大小·保證金

The survival layer: how much to risk per trade, how to cap a whole book by net delta and net vega, and how the margin engine — not your thesis — decides whether you live to trade tomorrow.

What it is

Sizing is the only input you control with certainty before a trade: not direction, not IV, not fills — just how much can this position cost me, and what fraction of capital is that. The discipline is to translate every structure into one number — dollars-at-risk — and hold it to a fixed fraction of the account. The trap that ends accounts is not being wrong; it is being wrong in oversize, where one tail event you "knew" was unlikely takes out a year of premium. Because short-premium P&L is negatively skewed (many small wins, rare large losses), naive position sizing systematically over-bets — the small-sample win streak feels like edge and invites scaling up right before the fat-tail draw.

Key math / intuition

Three sizing regimes, one per risk type — never one rule for all:

(1) Defined-risk (verticals, condors, flies, debit structures): risk is the explicit max loss = width − net credit, or the debit paid. Size off that. Default: 1–2% of account at risk per position; a beginner-conservative book caps total open defined-risk at ~5%. With a $50,000 account at 1%, that is $500 max loss per trade — e.g. one $5-wide put credit spread taken for $0.50 credit risks $450, which fits.

(2) Undefined-risk (naked puts/calls, short strangles, ratios): there is no contractual max loss, so you size off two limits at once — (a) the broker's BPR (Buying Power Reduction, the margin the position ties up, roughly 15–25% of notional for an equity short put plus the premium), and (b) a Kelly-lite tail haircut. Full Kelly (f* = edge ÷ odds) is far too aggressive for negatively-skewed short-vol: a single tail loss can be many multiples of the per-trade credit, so the variance term dominates. Practitioner rule: take at most a quarter to a half of Kelly, then stress the position to a 2–3σ adverse gap and require the account to survive that loss while still meeting maintenance margin. If a 2σ gap would trigger a margin call, the position is too big regardless of what Kelly says.

(3) Wheel / cash-secured: the binding number is capital-to-be-assigned = strike × 100 × contracts. A true CSP requires 100% of that in buying power; the naked ("margin") version frees most of it but converts a benign cash trade into an undefined-risk margin trade with assignment-driven calls. Size the Wheel as if you will be assigned every contract simultaneously in a crash — because in a correlated selloff you will. Never have more capital-to-be-assigned outstanding than you would willingly own as stock at those strikes.

Book-level limits (the part retail skips): two scalars govern the whole portfolio. Beta-weighted net delta normalizes every position's directional exposure to SPY-equivalent shares so a tech short put and an XLE covered call net against each other on one ruler; cap it (e.g. net beta-weighted delta < 0.3–0.5 × account ÷ SPY price, i.e. you are never implicitly more than ~half-invested in disguise). Net vega caps your exposure to a vol shock: a book that is short $2,000/vol-point loses $20,000 if VIX jumps 10 points — set a net-vega limit sized so a +10-point vol spike is survivable. These two numbers, checked daily, are what separate a portfolio from a pile of trades.

How to read it / where to get it free

Margin systems — know which one you are under. Reg-T is the Federal Reserve's Regulation T, which sets the initial requirement — 50% on stock; the strategy-based maintenance formulas that margin each option structure (and that margin a defined spread at its width) are FINRA Rule 4210. Together these give you fixed, strategy-based, predictable, blunt rules that do not reward correlation offsets. Portfolio Margin (PM) / SPAN-style risk-based (OCC's TIMS / Customer Portfolio Margin): margin = the portfolio's worst-case loss across a simulated price/vol grid, so well-hedged books can require dramatically less than Reg-T — but a concentrated short-vol book can require more, and the requirement moves in real time as vol rises. PM gates: FINRA minimum equity is $100,000 with full real-time intraday risk monitoring ($150,000 with only partial real-time monitoring); brokers set higher floors — Schwab ~$125,000, Interactive Brokers ~$110,000 (as-of 2026, est., confirm with your broker). PM gives leverage and takes it away exactly when vol spikes. Read your live BPR and maintenance-excess figures in your broker platform daily (tastytrade, IBKR, Schwab/thinkorswim all display them); beta-weighted delta is a one-click view in thinkorswim and tastytrade.

The gotcha

The short-gamma account-killer: assignment-driven margin calls and forced liquidation. When you are short options and spot moves hard against you into expiry, three things compound at once. (1) Your short option goes deep ITM, its margin requirement balloons (under PM it can multiply as vol explodes). (2) On assignment, a short put converts into a long-stock position requiring full purchase capital you may not have — the broker sees the shortfall and can liquidate your other positions to cover it. (3) Liquidation happens automatically, at the worst possible prices, often on an overnight gap before you can react — IBKR and others explicitly reserve the right to close positions if they project you cannot meet a potential assignment. This is why undefined-risk short premium is not "income": the failure mode is not a measured loss you choose to take, it is a forced, cascading deleveraging at the bottom. The defenses are structural, not heroic: cap capital-to-be-assigned to what you would own outright, keep maintenance excess large enough to survive a 2–3σ gap on your largest position, and prefer defined-risk wings on anything you cannot afford to be assigned. The market does not margin-call you for being wrong; it margin-calls you for being big and wrong at the worst moment.

Foundation

Data Sources — Free Map + How to Read 資料來源地圖

Every vol/flow module in this dashboard is admire-only until you can actually pull the number — here is the free source, the paid upgrade, how to read it, and the caveat for each data need.

What it is

A research map matching each data need to a concrete free source and its caveats. The single most important literacy skill embedded here: distinguish a measurement from an inference. IV, realized vol, OI, and earnings dates are measured (with model assumptions for IV). GEX/DEX, "dealer positioning," and max-pain implications are inferred from a model layered on stale OI data — they are coincident-to-lagging regime context, never a free mechanical signal. (Note the separate trap: max pain is a crowded post-hoc heuristic about where total OI is densest, which is NOT the same thing as pin risk — the real assignment uncertainty when spot sits near your short strike into expiry; see F4.) The second literacy skill: historical vs live. Most free Greeks/IV are delayed 15 minutes or end-of-day; trading off a 15-min-stale chain during a vol spike is how you get a fill nowhere near the screen.

Key math / intuition

How to read it / where to get it free

Build a once-a-day routine off three free anchors: (1) Barchart or your broker for IVR + HV-vs-IV on your watchlist (the short-vs-long-premium gate), (2) optioncharts.io for the SPX IV term structure and skew (so you read the right curve for calendars/skew trades), and (3) vixcentral.com for the VIX-futures curve (regime/contango context only). Layer GEX/DEX last and treat it strictly as regime color. The instant you intend to place a trade, abandon the free 15-min-delayed chain and price off your broker's live NBBO — the delayed mid is for study, the live book is for fills.

The gotcha

The two ways this layer quietly costs you money: (1) Treating an inference as a measurement. Free GEX dashboards render dealer-gamma profiles with clean flip levels that look like radar — but they are a model on top of stale OI, and two reputable providers will hand you opposite-signed GEX for the same tape. If you cannot explain the dealer-positioning assumption baked in, you are trading someone's guess as if it were a fact. (2) Trading off stale prices. A 15-minute-delayed chain during an open or a vol spike can be a dollar off the real mid on a wide strike; the screen says your spread is worth $0.50 and the live book fills you at $0.38. Free data is for forming the thesis and screening; the execution decision must touch live NBBO every time.

Foundation

Tax & Wash-Sale (Compact) 稅務與洗售

US-centric: the four tax rules that quietly rewrite your real edge — wash-sale on options, Section 1256 60/40, straddle loss-deferral, and constructive sales — and why after-tax math can flip the SPX-vs-SPY choice.

What it is

US tax rules only — non-US readers must substitute local rules (this is education, not tax advice; confirm with a professional). Pre-tax P&L is not your edge; after-tax, after-cost P&L is. For an active options trader four rules do most of the damage or the saving: the wash-sale rule (defers losses), Section 1256 60/40 (lowers the rate on index products), the straddle rules (defers losses on offsetting positions), and constructive sales (forces gain recognition on hedged appreciated stock). Each is a timing-or-rate lever, and together they can swing the rational instrument choice — most concretely, SPX vs SPY for the same S&P exposure.

Key math / intuition

(1) Wash-sale on options — IRC §1091. Sell a security (incl. an option, which the code explicitly counts as "stock or securities") at a loss and buy something substantially identical within the 61-day window (30 days before through 30 after, plus the sale day) and the loss is disallowed — not erased, but added to the replacement position's cost basis and pushed to the future. For options this is treacherous: rolling a losing put to a new strike/expiry, re-opening the same spread, or even a trade in your spouse's account or IRA can trip it — and a wash sale into an IRA permanently destroys the loss. "Substantially identical" for options is unsettled at the edges (different strikes/expiries may or may not count); when in doubt, sit out the 31 days or change the underlying.

(2) Section 1256 60/40 — the index advantage. "Section 1256 contracts" — broad-based index options (SPX, NDX, RUT, VIX), regulated futures, and options on futures — are marked to market at year-end and taxed 60% long-term / 40% short-term regardless of holding period, even on a one-day trade. Blended top federal rate ≈ 26.8% (60% × 20% + 40% × 37%) vs a flat 37% on short-term equity-option gains. Two side effects: mark-to-market means no wash-sale deferral applies to 1256 contracts (they are settled every year-end anyway), and losses can be carried back 3 years against prior 1256 gains (Form 6781). SPY options are ordinary equity options — none of this.

(3) Straddle rules — IRC §1092. Hold offsetting positions (a long and a short that substantially reduce each other's risk — e.g. a long stock hedged with a deep put, or two legs of certain spreads) and you cannot harvest the loss on one leg while the gain on the other leg stays unrealized: the loss is deferred to the extent of unrecognized gain in the offsetting position, plus the holding-period clock can be suspended and some carrying costs capitalized. The point is to stop traders from booking the loss leg this year and the gain leg next year. Reported on Form 6781.

(4) Constructive sales — IRC §1259. If you hold appreciated stock and then enter an offsetting position that effectively locks in the gain — e.g. a short-against-the-box, or certain zero-cost collars / forwards that eliminate nearly all upside and downside — the IRS can treat it as a constructive sale, forcing you to recognize the built-in gain now as if you sold. This is the wall that stops "hedge to defer the tax forever" on a big winner; a collar must leave meaningful risk in the position to avoid it. §1259 overrides the straddle deferral where it applies.

How to read it / where to get it free

Two practical reads. SPX vs SPY after tax: for the same S&P exposure, SPX is cash-settled, European-style (no physical-assignment / early-exercise risk (cash-settled), see F4), and 1256 60/40 — so for a short-term-heavy trader the after-tax rate alone (≈ 26.8% vs 37%) is a real, recurring edge that can dominate the slightly worse SPX bid/ask. SPY wins only if you specifically want shares, dividends, or sub-100-delta granularity. Loss harvesting: 1256 index products sidestep wash-sale entirely; single-name equity options do not — so plan year-end harvesting around the 61-day window on equities and freely on index 1256s. Free references: IRS Form 6781 + instructions, IRS Pub 550 (investment income, wash-sale and straddle sections), and reputable trader-tax explainers (Green Trader Tax). Keep broker 1099-B and a personal trade log — broker wash-sale tracking often misses cross-account and option-to-stock cases.

The gotcha

The quiet edge-killer is rolling losers and the wash-sale clock on equities. A trader who religiously rolls losing single-name puts "to defer the loss" may be manufacturing a chain of disallowed wash sales — the loss keeps getting absorbed into the next position's basis and never lands as a deduction this year, so the tax bill arrives on the wins while the offsetting losses sit frozen. Meanwhile the opposite mistake on the winning side — collaring a big appreciated stock too tightly to "lock it in and defer" — can trip a constructive sale and force the gain into income immediately. The throughline: tax rules tax timing and instrument, not just outcome. Trade SPX-style 1256 products to escape both wash-sale and the higher rate when you do not need shares; on single names, respect the 61-day window and leave real risk in any hedge on a winner. After-tax edge is the only edge that pays your bills.

03

The four levels — strategy by strategy

四個層級

Each strategy is a full teardown: ① 機制 mechanism · ② 損益 payoff · ③ 希臘字母 Greeks · ④ 適用情境 when it works · ⑤ 真實案例 real case · ⑥ 進場管理出場 playbook · ⑦ 選標的 fit screen · ⑧ 風險與卡點 risks. Payoff & Greeks charts are live (Black-Scholes rendered).

Trade now

Covered calls, cash-secured puts, the Wheel, and collars — on quality names you would genuinely hold.

Theory to internalize

Long calls/puts are long-vol & θ-negative: being right on direction but slow still loses. Know your vega sign before you click buy.

Avoid

Buying long-dated OTM options "for time"; selling puts on names you would not want to own at the strike.

L1 · Beginner

Long Call / Long Put 買權／賣權（做多權利金）

Pay a fixed debit for convex, time-limited directional exposure — built for a HIGH-conviction move that arrives BEFORE theta and a possible IV-crush erode the premium.

Skill-dependent
Prereq: Greeks · IV & surface · Vega-sign axis

① 機制 Mechanism

You pay a debit (the premium) to OWN the right — not the obligation — to buy (call) or sell (put) 100 shares at strike K until expiry. This is the long-premium, long-vol half of L1: you are LONG optionality, so you are long vega and SHORT theta — time and falling IV work against you every day. Your downside is capped at the debit; your upside is convex (a long call's delta rises toward +1.0 as it goes in-the-money). Top-down: think of it as renting leverage and a stop-loss in one ticket — the premium is the rent, and the rent is most expensive exactly when you most want to pay it (high IV before a catalyst).

② 損益結構 Payoff & breakevens

Unlimitedmax profit (call)
−debitmax loss (= premium)
K + debitbreakeven · POP ≈ 35–45%

③ 希臘字母特徵 Greeks signature

Δ positive (call) / negative (put), magnitude ~0.30–0.60 at entry for a near-ATM/OTM strike. Γ positive (delta accelerates in your favor on a move) — the source of convexity. Θ negative — the daily bleed, which ACCELERATES into expiry for ATM options (theta is roughly proportional to 1/√DTE). V positive — you profit if IV rises, lose if it falls. The killer dynamic: hold the ATM option you bought 30 DTE ago and the last two weeks of theta decay are LARGER than the first two — the bleed you signed up for back-loads right when you can least afford it. The curve below shows delta ramping from ~0.5 toward 1.0 as spot rises — that convexity is what you paid for.

④ 適用情境 When it works / fails

The ONE cell: a strong directional view × LOW-to-moderate IV (you are buying vega — do not overpay) × ideally a catalyst you expect to surprise consensus. It FAILS in the most common retail setup: buying calls when IVR is already high before earnings — even if you are right on direction, the post-event IV crush can leave you flat or down. Be explicit: a long option is a bet that forward realized vol > the IV you paid, AND that the move comes fast enough to beat theta. Most long options lose because the underlying chops sideways: realized < implied, and theta wins.

⑤ 真實案例 Real-world case

NVDA, post-split June–Oct 2024. After the 10-for-1 split took effect 2024-06-10 (shares re-based to ~$120), NVDA dipped to the high-$90s in early August then rebounded above $135 by October (prices verified, Macrotrends/StockScan). A 60-DTE ATM long call bought near the August low would have multiplied several-fold on the rebound — convexity working. The LESSON (and the honest counter-case): the same call bought at the June high, ahead of the August selloff, would have decayed toward zero before the rebound arrived. Same name, same direction-eventually — timing and theta decided everything. (Option premia reconstructed/est.; underlying prices verified.)

⑥ 進場／管理／出場 Playbook

DTE window45–90 (buy time, not lotto)
Profit target+50–100% of debit
Stop / time-stop−50% debit; exit by 21 DTE
SizingDefined-risk: ≤ 1–2% of account per ticket (max loss = full debit)
Defend triggerActionResulting structureNew risk
Up 50%, thesis intactSell an OTM call against itVertical debit spreadCaps further upside
Approaching 21 DTE, still rightRoll out to next 45–60 DTENew long call, fresh theta clockPay more premium / vega
Thesis broken / −50%Close, take the lossFlatNone (realize loss)
Mini ticket (illustrative) — Buy 1× ATM call, spot 100, K=100, 60 DTE, IV 30%, debit ≈ $3.40/sh = $340. Max loss $340. Breakeven 103.40. Target +$170 (50%). Stop −$170. Time-stop 21 DTE. (Full worked TICKET lives in L1-3 CSP.)

⑦ 選標的 Fit-target screen

Screen on Barchart Stock Screener (free) or OptionStrat: option OI > 500 at target strike, bid/ask < 8% of mid, 30-day IVR < 40, a clean catalyst > 21 days out (avoid buying into an already-priced earnings IV ramp). Candidates as-of 2026-06-17 (est.): liquid large-caps with tight chains such as AAPL and AMD — deep OI, penny-wide strikes near ATM.

⑧ 風險與卡點 Risks & the gotcha

The gotcha is the triple-whammy of being right and still losing: correct direction, but (a) too slow → theta ate it, (b) bought high IV → crush ate it, (c) OTM strike → delta too low to catch the move. The path matters as much as the destination.

Honest edge — the falsifiable metric is forward realized vol vs the IV you paid. If, over your hold, realized vol < entry IV, you were structurally overcharged regardless of direction. Verdict: Skill-dependent — edge exists only if you can time vol/catalysts AND consistently buy cheap IV; most retail long-option books bleed because they do neither.

L1 · Beginner

Covered Call 掩護性買權

Long 100 shares with a short OTM call overlay — built for a stock you'd hold anyway in a FLAT-to-modestly-up, range-bound tape where you accept a capped top for income.

Genuine (modest)
Prereq: Greeks · Assignment & pin · Parity

① 機制 Mechanism

You own 100 shares and SELL one OTM call against them, collecting a credit. Read the risk correctly (correction #2): a covered call's DOMINANT exposure is the long-stock delta (~+0.7 to +1.0) — it is fundamentally a long equity position with a capped top, plus a SMALL short-vol/short-theta overlay. It is NOT primarily "a short-vol bet." The short call only trims a few delta and adds the premium cushion. By put-call parity (F3) the payoff equals a short cash-secured put at the same strike — but the risk that actually drives your P&L is equity beta, not the option.

② 損益結構 Payoff & breakevens

(K − cost) + creditmax profit (capped at K)
cost − creditmax loss (stock to 0, cushioned)
cost basis − creditbreakeven · POP ≈ 60–70%

③ 希臘字母特徵 Greeks signature

Net Δ strongly positive: +1.0 from stock minus the call's delta (≈ 0.25–0.35 for a 30-delta OTM call) → net ~+0.65 to +0.75. THAT is the position — long equity. Γ slightly negative, Θ slightly positive (the small income overlay), V slightly negative. The delta curve below shows the giveaway: it sits high and flat near +1.0 on the downside (you eat almost all of a selloff) and ROLLS OVER toward zero above the strike (your upside is capped). The shape literally draws "good in a chop, bad in a crash, bad in a melt-up."

④ 適用情境 When it works / fails

The ONE cell: flat-to-modestly-up view × elevated IVR (richer call premium) × no near-term blow-out catalyst, on a stock you are content to own and to have called away at K. It FAILS in TWO directions: in a big rally you are capped and watch the stock run past K (opportunity cost), and in a big selloff the tiny premium barely cushions a long-equity loss. The credit is a thin shock-absorber, not a hedge.

⑤ 真實案例 Real-world case

The capped-rally cost, two ways. (1) Index proxy: JEPI (JPMorgan Equity Premium Income, a covered-call ETF) returned +12.56% total return in calendar 2024 vs the S&P 500's +25.02% — a ~12.5-point drag in a strong-up year, the structural capped-top tax made visible (verified: FinanceCharts / stockanalysis). (2) Single name: writing calls on NVDA after its 2024-06-10 split (re-based ~$120) into the rebound above $135 by October would have meant assignment near your strike while the stock ran — you'd have collected a small credit and missed the bulk of the move. LESSON: covered calls monetize boredom, and the bill comes due exactly in the rallies you most wanted. (Single-name premia est.; ETF returns and NVDA prices verified.)

⑥ 進場／管理／出場 Playbook

ParamDefault
Entry IVR gate> 30 (sell richer vol)
Delta / strike0.20–0.30 short call (OTM)
DTE window30–45
Profit targetclose call at 50% of credit
Stop / time-stopmanage at 21 DTE; roll or let assign
SizingCapital-to-be-held: 100 shares × price = full equity exposure; size as a stock position
Defend triggerActionResulting structureNew risk
Stock rallies through K, want to keep sharesRoll call UP & OUT for net creditHigher-strike CC, later expiryStill capped; extends duration
Stock rallies, OK to sellLet it be assigned at KFlat (shares called away)Tax on gain; opportunity cost
Stock drops hardRoll call DOWN for more credit (cautiously)Lower-strike CCCaps the rebound — "deferring a loss" trap
Mini ticket (illustrative) — Own 100 sh @ 100. Sell 1× 105 call, 35 DTE, IV 30%, credit ≈ $1.20/sh = $120. Max profit if called: $5.00 appreciation + $1.20 credit = $620. Breakeven 98.80. Net delta ≈ +0.72. (Full worked TICKET lives in L1-3 CSP.)

⑦ 選標的 Fit-target screen

Screen on Barchart "Covered Call" screener (free): stock you'd hold anyway, weekly/monthly chain with OI > 1000 at the ~0.25-delta strike, bid/ask < 6% of mid, 30-day IVR > 30, NO earnings inside the DTE (assignment/ex-div risk). Candidates as-of 2026-06-17 (est.): high-liquidity, lower-beta quality names like KO or MSFT — deep chains, manageable swings, you're happy to own.

⑧ 風險與卡點 Risks & the gotcha

The gotcha is misreading what you own: traders treat a covered call as "income," forget it is ~+0.7 delta of long equity, and get hurt by the STOCK, not the option. Secondary: early assignment on the call around ex-dividend if it goes ITM (see F4) — the dividend-capture exercise can strip your shares before expiry.

Honest edge — the metric is option premium captured vs the upside you forfeited, measured over a full cycle. On quality names in elevated-IVR chop the captured premium beats the forfeited tail; in trending/melt-up tape it loses badly. Verdict: Genuine-but-modest — a real volatility-risk-premium harvest on the right name and regime, but it is an equity position first and an income trade a distant second.

L1 · Beginner

Cash-Secured Put 現金擔保賣權

Sell an OTM put fully collateralized by cash — built to get PAID to set a limit-buy on a stock you want to own, in a flat-to-up tape with elevated IVR.

Genuine (modest)
Prereq: Parity · Assignment & pin · Sizing & margin

① 機制 Mechanism

You SELL one put at strike K and set aside K × 100 in cash to buy the shares if assigned. This is the short-premium, short-vol side of L1: short vega, positive theta, you collect a credit and profit from time/IV decay. The classic shorthand "CSP = synthetic covered call" is true ONLY under put-call parity (F3): the expiry payoffs match. Qualify it always (correction #3): (a) collateral/BPR differs — CSP ties up cash (or BPR on margin); CC ties up the shares; (b) assignment delivers cash-into-shares for the CSP vs the CC writer already holding shares; (c) dividends & early-exercise diverge — ex-dividend is the SHORT-CALL trap: the CC writer earns the dividend but faces ex-div early CALL assignment because the call holder exercises to capture the dividend. The CSP writer's SHORT PUT is different: it is assigned early mainly when it goes deep ITM with low / near-zero extrinsic value and interest/carry makes it optimal for the put holder to exercise — exercising frees the put holder to collect interest on the strike cash, so it is a rates-driven event, not a dividend one. (The CSP writer, holding the cash, is the one earning that interest until assigned.) Same picture, different plumbing.

② 損益結構 Payoff & breakevens

creditmax profit (= premium)
(K − credit) × 100max loss (stock to 0)
K − creditbreakeven · POP ≈ 70–80%

③ 希臘字母特徵 Greeks signature

Δ positive (you want the stock up/flat), magnitude = the put's delta, e.g. +0.20–0.30 for a ~0.25-delta short put. Γ negative (losses accelerate as it goes ITM — the short-vol signature), Θ positive (your daily friend), V negative (an IV spike hurts before it helps). The payoff above is a mirror of a long stock with a capped top — same shape as the covered call, as parity predicts. The risk that bites is the negative gamma in a fast drop.

④ 適用情境 When it works / fails

The ONE cell: neutral-to-bullish on a name you actually want to own × elevated IVR (rich put premium, often fattened by skew) × no binary catalyst inside the DTE. It WORKS as a paid limit order in chop and grind-up. It FAILS in a gap-down / vol-expansion: negative gamma means the loss outruns the credit fast, and you get assigned a falling knife at K while the stock trades well below your breakeven.

⑤ 真實案例 Real-world case

Why "short premium" is not free — Volmageddon, Feb 5 2018. The VIX roughly doubled to close near 37 (intraday ~50), the S&P 500 fell ~4.1% in a day, and the short-vol ETP XIV lost ~96% of its indicative value and was terminated (verified: Macroption, SixFigureInvesting, CBOE). XIV was a leveraged institutional cousin of "collect-the-premium," but the lesson transfers exactly: a book of naked short puts in a one-day vol-expansion behaves like XIV in miniature — negative gamma turns a steady income stream into a step-function loss. The CSP's saving grace vs XIV is that it is cash-secured and single-name: your worst case is owning the stock at K, not a wipeout. LESSON: size for the gap, not the average week. (VIX/XIV figures verified; CSP P&L illustrative.)

⑥ 進場／管理／出場 Playbook

ParamDefault
Entry IVR gate> 30 (don't sell cheap puts)
Delta / strike0.20–0.30 short put (OTM)
DTE window30–45

Stop / time-stopmanage at 21 DTE; defend if tested

Won 50% earlyBuy back, redeployFlat, free up collateralNone
Worked TRADE TICKET (illustrative, normalized) — Underlying: liquid quality stock, spot $100.00. IVR: 42. Structure: Sell 1× 95-strike PUT. DTE: 35 (no earnings inside window). Contracts: 1 (= 100 sh of exposure). Net credit: $1.50/sh = $150. Collateral / BPR: cash-secured = $9,500 set aside (Reg-T margin BPR would be ≈ $1,900–$2,400 instead — lower capital, higher assignment-margin risk). Max loss: $9,350 (stock to 0; = (95 − 1.50) × 100). Breakeven: $93.50. POP: ≈ 78% (1 − entry delta, est.). Profit target: +$75 (buy back at 50% of credit, ≈ $0.75). Stop / defense: if spot < $95 and you DON'T want assignment, roll down & out for a net credit; if you DO want it, accept assignment at cost basis $93.50. Roll trigger: short put delta > 0.50 or 21 DTE reached. Exit: close at 50% profit, OR take assignment and roll into a covered call (the Wheel, L1-4). Return-on-capital if it expires worthless: $150 / $9,500 ≈ 1.6% in 35 days (cash-secured) ≈ ~16% annualized — before the tail-loss reserve. (Premia reconstructed/est.; structure & margin mechanics correct as of 2026-06-17.)

⑦ 選標的 Fit-target screen

Screen on Barchart "Naked Put / CSP" screener (free) or OptionStrat: a name you'd own at K, put OI > 1000 at the ~0.25-delta strike, bid/ask < 6% of mid, 30-day IVR > 30, NO earnings inside DTE, beta you can stomach in a gap. Candidates as-of 2026-06-17 (est.): SCHD (low-beta dividend ETF, you'd happily own) and MSFT (deep liquid chain).

⑧ 風險與卡點 Risks & the gotcha

The gotcha is negative-gamma asymmetry: you win small and often, then a single gap-down hands you a big assignment loss — the "tail that eats the year." Selling puts ONLY on names you don't actually want is the cardinal sin; assignment then forces you to own a stock you dislike at a losing price.

Honest edge — the metric is credit collected vs the realized loss on assigned positions, summed over a full cycle (N ≥ ~30 trades). On quality names in elevated IVR this nets a genuine volatility-risk-premium; on junk or in a vol-regime break it is negative. Verdict: Genuine-but-modest — same VRP harvest as the covered call (parity), with the same one rule: only sell puts where assignment is a feature, not a disaster.

L1 · Beginner

The Wheel 輪轉策略

A repeatable cycle — sell CSP → if assigned, hold stock and sell CC → if called away, sell CSP again — built to harvest premium continuously on a name you're content to own through chop.

Genuine (modest)
Prereq: CSP · Covered Call · Assignment & pin · Sizing

① 機制 Mechanism

The Wheel chains the two L1 short-premium trades into a loop. Phase 1: sell a cash-secured put on a name you'd own. Phase 2 (if assigned): you now hold 100 shares at cost basis = K − credit; immediately sell an OTM covered call against them. Phase 3 (if called away): shares leave at the call strike; return to Phase 1. Top-down: it is a systematic VRP-harvesting machine wrapped around an equity you're happy to warehouse. Critically, by parity (F3) Phase 1 and Phase 3's CC are the SAME short-vol exposure in different clothing — the Wheel is just "be short an option on this name, always."

② 損益結構 Payoff & breakevens

The Wheel has no single payoff — it alternates between two distinct single-expiry states, so we render BOTH. Conceptually each state is a payoff you've already seen; chained, your effective cost basis ratchets DOWN by each premium collected as long as you keep cycling without a large adverse move.

State A — Entry: cash-secured put. Short the 95 put; you profit if the stock stays above the breakeven (K − credit) and either keep the credit or get assigned the shares cheaply.

State B — After assignment: covered call. You now hold the shares and sell the 105 call against them; capped above K, full downside on the warehoused stock below.

The cycle: State A → assignment → State B → called away → State A. Each loop, the running breakeven on the position drops by the premium harvested.

PhasePositionYou profit if…Trigger to next phase
1 · Sell CSPshort put + cashstock ≥ K at expiryassigned → Phase 2
2 · Covered Calllong 100 sh + short callstock chops near/above basiscalled away → Phase 3
3 · Resetcash freed—sell new CSP → Phase 1
Σ premiumscumulative income
stock → 0max loss (own falling shares)
basis − Σ creditrunning breakeven

③ 希臘字母特徵 Greeks signature

Always net long delta (you're structurally bullish/owner-minded), negative gamma, positive theta, negative vega — the persistent short-vol fingerprint, identical in sign across all three phases; only the magnitude shifts (highest delta in Phase 2 when you hold shares). You are continuously paid theta for carrying gamma risk on this one name.

④ 適用情境 When it works / fails

The ONE cell: a range-bound-to-grinding-up quality name × persistently elevated IVR × willingness to own it for the long haul. It WORKS beautifully in sideways chop — you collect premium in both directions. It FAILS in a secular downtrend: you get assigned, sell calls below your sinking basis, and the premiums never catch the decline. The Wheel on a declining name slowly converts a stock loss into a "busy" stock loss.

⑤ 真實案例 Real-world case

The roll-down-and-out "deferring a loss" trap. Suppose you sold a 95 CSP on a name at spot 100; it gaps to 82 on bad news. The Wheel discipline says either (a) accept assignment at basis 93.50 and sell calls, or (b) roll the put down-and-out. The trap: rolling repeatedly for tiny net credits FEELS like "I haven't lost," but you've simply parked an unrealized loss and added duration + tail risk each time. Mirror of the JEPI lesson (L1-2): a covered-call/Wheel program returned +12.56% in 2024 vs S&P +25.02% — fine in chop, painful when the underlying trends against the cap. LESSON: the Wheel is income on a name you'd own, NOT a way to avoid ever booking a loss. (JEPI/SPY verified; roll example illustrative.)

⑥ 進場／管理／出場 Playbook

ParamDefault
Entry IVR gate> 30 each leg
Delta / strikeCSP 0.25–0.30; CC 0.20–0.30
DTE window30–45 per leg
Profit target50% credit per leg, then re-sell
Stop / time-stopdecision at 21 DTE; thesis-stop on the NAME
SizingCapital-to-be-assigned: 1 wheel per ~10–15% of account max; never wheel your whole book on one name
Defend triggerActionResulting structureNew risk
CSP tested, still want the stockTAKE assignment (don't reflexively roll)Long shares → Phase 2 CCLong equity drawdown
CSP tested, name thesis intact, not readyRoll down & out ONLY for net credit, ONCELower CSP, later expiryDeferring-a-loss trap if repeated
Holding shares, want to keep them in a rallyRoll CC up & out for creditHigher-strike CCStill capped
NAME thesis brokenExit the wheel entirely, book itFlatRealized loss — correct choice
Mini ticket (illustrative) — Phase 1: sell 95 CSP, 35 DTE, credit $150 (see L1-3 full ticket). If assigned: basis $93.50; Phase 2: sell 105 OTM CC, 35 DTE, credit $110 → new basis $92.40. If called away at 105: total profit ≈ $150 + $110 + ($105−$93.50)×100 = $1,410 over two cycles. (Full worked TICKET lives in L1-3 CSP.)

⑦ 選標的 Fit-target screen

Screen on Barchart (free) for both legs: a name you'd own for years, IVR > 30, weekly+monthly chains with OI > 1000 at the 0.25-delta strikes, bid/ask < 6% of mid, beta < 1.3, NOT in a confirmed downtrend (price > rising 200-day SMA). Candidates as-of 2026-06-17 (est.): SCHD, KO — low-drama, deep chains, comfortable to warehouse.

⑧ 風險與卡點 Risks & the gotcha

The gotcha is the roll-down-and-out loop disguising a deteriorating thesis: endless small credits mask a stock you should have exited. Secondary: ex-div early assignment on the Phase-2 CC (F4), and concentration — wheeling one name turns a diversified account into a single-stock bet.

Honest edge — the metric is Σ premiums vs the mark-to-market loss on the warehoused shares, over a full regime. Positive only on names that chop/grind up; negative on secular decliners. Verdict: Genuine-but-modest — a disciplined VRP harvest, undone by the single most common error: refusing to ever book a loss on the underlying.

L1 · Beginner

Protective Put & Collar 保護性賣權與項圈

Buy a put to insure long stock (protective put), or finance that put by also selling an OTM call (collar) — built to cap downside on a position you must hold, accepting drag or a capped top.

Genuine (hedge)
Prereq: Greeks · IV & skew · Parity

① 機制 Mechanism

Two related hedges on a long-stock position. Protective put: own 100 shares + BUY one OTM put — a paid insurance policy; the put debit is the premium and floors your loss at the strike. Collar: the protective put PLUS a short OTM call whose credit pays for some or all of the put — you finance the insurance by selling away your upside above the call strike. By parity (F3) a collar is a vertical-spread payoff: long stock + long put + short call ≈ a bull risk-reversal capped on both ends. Top-down: protective put = "insurance you pay cash for"; collar = "insurance you pay for with your upside."

② 損益結構 Payoff & breakevens

K_call − cost ± netmax profit (capped at call)
cost − K_put ± netmax loss (floored at put)
cost ± net premiumbreakeven · defined range

③ 希臘字母特徵 Greeks signature

Net Δ positive but reduced vs naked stock (the long put trims downside delta; the short call trims upside delta) — a collar's net delta sits well below +1.0, e.g. ~+0.4–0.6. The long put adds positive gamma and positive vega (you GAIN if vol spikes in a selloff — the hedge works hardest exactly when you need it); the short call in a collar partly offsets that vega. Net theta is small (long-put bleed vs short-call income roughly net in a zero-cost collar). The payoff above shows the signature shape: a flat floor below 95, a flat ceiling above 108, and stock-like P&L in between.

④ 適用情境 When it works / fails

The ONE cell: you must hold a concentrated/appreciated long position (tax lock-up, RSU, conviction) × you fear a near-term drawdown × IV/skew matters — puts are expensive when you most want them (high IVR/steep skew makes the protective put costly; the collar's short call helps fund it). Protective put WORKS as cheap-ish insurance when IVR is LOW; it's a DRAG when IVR is high. Collar WORKS to hedge near-free but FAILS to capture a big rally (capped). It FAILS as a standalone alpha trade — it is risk management, not a money-maker.

⑤ 真實案例 Real-world case

The price of insurance, made concrete. Index put protection (the closest verifiable analog) historically runs a meaningful annual drag: continuously rolling ~5% OTM SPX puts has cost on the order of 2–4%/yr in premium in normal regimes (est., widely documented in CBOE/academic put-protection studies) — i.e., insurance is NOT free, and over time the cumulative premium often exceeds the crashes it covers. That is exactly why the collar exists: in a high-IV environment like early 2020 or the 2018 vol spike (VIX ~37 close on 2018-02-05, verified), a naked protective put was very expensive, so hedgers sold an OTM call to finance it — accepting a capped top to make the floor near-zero-cost. LESSON: choose the hedge by IV regime — pay cash for the put when vol is cheap; finance it via a collar when vol (and skew) is rich. (Put-protection drag = est. from public studies; VIX 2018 verified.)

⑥ 進場／管理／出場 Playbook

ParamDefault
Entry IVR gateput: prefer IVR < 40; collar: any (finance it)
Delta / strikeput 0.15–0.25 (5–10% OTM); call 0.20–0.30
DTE window60–120 (longer = cheaper per-day insurance)
Profit targetN/A — it's a hedge; judge by drawdown avoided
Stop / time-stoproll the put before 21 DTE; re-collar quarterly
SizingHedge ratio: protect the % of the position you can't afford to lose, not 100% reflexively
Defend triggerActionResulting structureNew risk
Stock rallies to the call strike (collar)Roll call up & out for creditWider collar, more upsideRe-introduces some downside cost
